Discussion Name:
Should School be Mandatory?

1. Thesis:
School should be mandatory.

  1.1. Pro:
  Mandatory schooling supports the social development and emotional well-being of children.

    1.1.1. Con:
    Many students undergo traumatic experiences in school (p. 28 [1]); making school mandatory forces them to be exposed to such negative incidents.

      1.1.1.1. Con:
      Negative social experiences are a reality of life. It is better that children become acquainted [2] with them through mandatory school than face unpleasant surprises when they are older.

        1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
        Learning to deal with bullies in school can help children develop social skills [3] to successfully deal with unpleasant people and situations.

        1.1.1.1.2. Con:
        Students, particularly those from underprivileged backgrounds, are often exposed to trauma and stress [4] outside of school; it is unacceptable to subject students to this in school as well.

      1.1.1.2. Pro:
      Many students experience or witness violence in schools. 21% of schools in the US reported one or more incidents of serious violent crime [5] during the 2017–2018 school year.

        1.1.1.2.1. Con:
        Students may be more likely [6] to experience traumatic violent events if they are excluded from school, as they may turn to criminal gangs to find social support.

        1.1.1.2.2. Pro:
        School safety is impacted by the ethnicity of students and the location of their school; students in urban areas and minority students [7] are less likely to feel safe in school, which compounds existing inequalities.

      1.1.1.3. Con:
      In all 50 US states, school employees are obligated to report signs of child abuse [8]; schools often play an indispensable role in protecting children.

        1.1.1.3.1. Con:
        Nearly 10% of students are victims of sexual misconduct perpetrated by an educator (pp. 17–18 [9]).

        1.1.1.3.2. Pro:
        Schools support preventative education programs, which are an effective intervention against child abuse (p. 129 [10]).

        1.1.1.3.3. Con:
        In some cases, overzealous schools and teachers have filed inaccurate or unwarranted reports [11] with Child Protective Services, which can severely damage children and parents alike.

      1.1.1.4. Pro:
      Many students are bullied at school, which can make their lives miserable and lead to lasting negative effects [12].

        1.1.1.4.1. Pro:
        Being bullied can lead to lower academic performance [13], chronic depression, and anxiety.

        1.1.1.4.2. Pro:
        Homophobic and transphobic bullying and harassment [14] have become so widespread that many schools are unsafe for LGBTQ+ students.

          1.1.1.4.2.1. Pro:
          Many teachers are unable or unwilling [15] to address bullying based on sexual orientation or gender identity.

        1.1.1.4.3. Con:
        Almost 60% of teens report being subjected to cyberbullying [16] outside of the school setting, indicating that this is symptomatic of a wider social problem.

    1.1.2. Con:
    Students dealing with emotional or social issues, such as social anxiety and depression [17], can find it difficult to thrive in the typical, structured school setting.

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      Mandatory school systems typically offer special education alternatives [18] for students with learning or behavioral issues.

        1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
        Special education teachers are specifically trained [19] to help students overcome emotional, social, or learning issues.

        1.1.2.1.2. Con:
        Special education is often underfunded [20] in mandatory school systems.

        1.1.2.1.3. Pro:
        Mandatory schooling is supported by a number of legal protections [21] that ensure children with additional needs are well taught instead of ignored.

        1.1.2.1.4. Con:
        The flawed process for identifying students [22] with special education needs prevents many students from receiving an adequate education.

      1.1.2.2. Pro:
      Forcing determinedly disruptive or anti-education students to attend school degrades [23] the education of their classmates.

        1.1.2.2.1. Con:
        Mandatory schooling can accommodate disruptive students [24] by reassigning them to diversionary schools or having them complete their education at home via remote learning.

        1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
        Teachers who are forced to deal with repeatedly disruptive students are more likely to experience burnout and change professions (p. 5 [25]).

      1.1.2.3. Pro:
      School can fuel [26] students' anxiety, encouraging a toxic grind [27] culture that is often ignored [28].

        1.1.2.3.1. Pro:
        The importance of placement testing [29] within some mandatory school systems has been identified as a significant source of student stress and anxiety.

        1.1.2.3.2. Con:
        The long-term stresses associated with dropping out of school decrease life expectancy by nine years [30].

      1.1.2.4. Con:
      If school is mandatory, parents with concerns about the negative social aspects of school can choose to homeschool [31] their children.

        1.1.2.4.1. Pro:
        There continue to be frequent school shootings [32] in the US, so home-schooled children are safer.

        1.1.2.4.2. Con:
        Students that are schooled at home do not have the same opportunities to socialize as those who attend school (p. 29 [33]).

          1.1.2.4.2.1. Con:
          Home-schooled children [34] can socialize in support groups and co-ops or attend extracurricular activities at local schools.

          1.1.2.4.2.2. Pro:
          Larger classes offer students opportunities for essential peer learning [35] partnerships, unlike the solitary learning environment utilized in homeschooling.

          1.1.2.4.2.3. Con:
          Home-schooled students can participate in community events [36] more easily than their peers, as they are not confined to school during the week.

    1.1.3. Pro:
    Mandatory schooling can foster the overall well-being [37] of a child, educating and preparing them for life.

      1.1.3.1. Con:
      Mandatory schooling often unnecessarily promotes moral and ethical values rather than encouraging students to choose values for themselves [38].

        1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
        Many teachers are unable to refrain from imposing their moral values [39] on students and fail to assist students in making their own moral evaluations.

        1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
        In many modern societies, compulsory education is instrumental in the imposition of hegemonic moral values (pp. 148, 163 [40]).

          1.1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
          Compulsory education in the West originated with the Protestant Reformation and its associated moral values (p. 15 [41]).

        1.1.3.1.3. Con:
        Moral education in mandatory school is necessary. School-age children are not yet capable [42] of selecting their own moral and ethical values, so they often absorb undesirable or antisocial values from other sources, such as family; mandatory schooling can help to correct these.

        1.1.3.1.4. Con:
        Teaching necessarily imparts values and norms (p. 4 [43]). Having a mandatory common school system ensures that all citizens are introduced to the same values and norms.

          1.1.3.1.4.1. Con:
          There can be great variation in norms and values between communities and cultures (pp. 27, 42 [44]). It is inappropriate for mandatory schooling to homogenize values and norms in diverse populations.

          1.1.3.1.4.2. Pro:
          It is essential that children be taught a set of moral values [45] so they can function in line with the expectations of society.

      1.1.3.2. Con:
      Mandatory school systems have not adapted [46] to the ways that the world has changed and subsequently don't prepare young people for the modern world.

        1.1.3.2.1. Pro:
        Many essential digital life skills [47] are not included in mandatory school curricula. The demands of school, thus, impose a high opportunity cost [48] on students who would prefer to learn these skills.

        1.1.3.2.2. Con:
        If school were not mandatory, preparation for the modern world would be contingent on a family's resources alone, making it far more likely that some students would fall behind [49].

      1.1.3.3. Pro:
      Social-emotional learning [50] (SEL) is increasingly incorporated in classrooms. This teaches students to be more socially aware and better prepares them to deal with stress.

        1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
        In-school play promotes the development of social and emotional learning (SEL), language skills, and creativity (p. 93 [51]).

          1.1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
          Play is important for learning to deal with unpredictable situations [52].

        1.1.3.3.2. Pro:
        SEL can prepare [53] students to live in more diverse communities by teaching them to treat others with empathy and kindness.

        1.1.3.3.3. Pro:
        Well-timed SEL [54] can support students through difficult periods of their lives, providing a bulwark against peer pressure and mental health stress.

        1.1.3.3.4. Con:
        At times, SEL is misused to regulate [55] rather than empower students. This can have profoundly negative effects on students that are already at risk of marginalization.

      1.1.3.4. Pro:
      Schooling offers strategies, systems, and support networks that help children find positive pathways to adulthood (p. 133 [56]).

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        Schools can easily demoralize [57] students with learning difficulties, such as ADHD, and make them more likely to drop out (p. 838 [58]).

        1.1.3.4.2. Pro:
        Student participation in school events and activities is associated with higher life satisfaction and greater reported happiness (pp. 93–94 [59]).

      1.1.3.5. Con:
      School programs focused on the well-being of children can end up doing more harm than good [60].

        1.1.3.5.1. Pro:
        Increased school security and zero-tolerance behavior policies intended to protect students often have negative repercussions (p. 4 [61]).

        1.1.3.5.2. Con:
        Even if some initiatives fall short of the mark, schools cannot ignore the important role they play in student well-being [62].

      1.1.3.6. Pro:
      Children unable to attend school during the coronavirus pandemic [63] displayed significant increases in emotional and behavioral difficulties.

        1.1.3.6.1. Pro:
        School closures due to the coronavirus pandemic increased the risk of social isolation [64] in children, which is linked to mental health problems.

        1.1.3.6.2. Pro:
        In response to the coronavirus pandemic, many schools adopted remote technology, allowing children to both learn and socialize virtually [65] to improve their overall wellness.

        1.1.3.6.3. Con:
        Coronavirus-related school closures also had positive effects on student mental health and well-being as they experienced reduced pressure [66] from schoolwork and bullying.

    1.1.4. Pro:
    The secondary socialization [67] that children undergo in school can help them develop social competencies that will benefit them for life (p. 85 [68]).

      1.1.4.1. Pro:
      Mandatory school provides students with access to teachers and counselors, who act as influential role models [69].

        1.1.4.1.1. Con:
        Adults outside of schools can be equally positive role models [70] for children.

        1.1.4.1.2. Pro:
        School guidance counselors [71] can play a positive role in students' academic, personal, and career development.

          1.1.4.1.2.1. Con:
          US schools seldom [72] achieve the recommended student-counsellor ratio advised by the American School Counselor Association (ASCA).

          1.1.4.1.2.2. Pro:
          Some schools have partnerships with nonprofit organizations [73] to provide post-secondary planning and counseling to students.

          1.1.4.1.2.3. Pro:
          Interaction with effective school counselors can increase student graduation rates, especially among low-achieving and low-income [74] students (p. 1 [75]).

          1.1.4.1.2.4. Con:
          Most people who graduate from public high schools in the US do not feel that the advice of their guidance counselors proved to be of any value [76].

      1.1.4.2. Con:
      School can introduce children to others who may be a bad influence on them (p. 830 [77]).

        1.1.4.2.1. Con:
        It is better that children have the opportunity to meet those unlike them than to be restricted to their parents' social circle, as would likely occur if they did not attend school.

        1.1.4.2.2. Con:
        Schools can create well-designed networks that greatly uplift students (p. 5 [78]); meeting others often benefits students more than it hinders them.

        1.1.4.2.3. Pro:
        Teens are particularly susceptible to peer pressure around drinking [79]. The social hierarchies [80] established in school may exacerbate this pressure, encouraging teens to drink to fit in.

      1.1.4.3. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling allows children to meet and play with other children [81], which leads to a number of benefits.

        1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
        Play is essential to people's mental health [82]; mandatory schooling protects and institutes the importance of play [83] in a way that other education systems may not.

          1.1.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          The cooperation, friendship, and fun that children experience during play have intrinsic value [84] and are an important source of meaning and joy for them.

        1.1.4.3.2. Con:
        Children can play just as easily outside of school (p. 6 [85]).

          1.1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
          Schools are often poorly equipped for play [86]. Children would be better off playing elsewhere, like local parks or woods.

            1.1.4.3.2.1.1. Con:
            Schools provide a much safer environment for children to play in than public, unmonitored spaces (pp. 1, 4 [87]).

            1.1.4.3.2.1.2. Pro:
            Excessive restrictions and measures intended to keep children safe in school can make them less capable of managing risk on their own and, thus, less safe in the long term (p. 3142 [88]).

          1.1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
          Outdoor play is associated with substantial health benefits for children (p. 2 [89]).

          1.1.4.3.2.3. Con:
          For many children, school is one of the few places and times where they get to play with other children (p. 23 [90]).

          1.1.4.3.2.4. Con:
          Schools are more conducive to play, as there are many children of the same age group in one location at the same time.

        1.1.4.3.3. Con:
        Schools usually have little time and space for free play, and have increasingly cut back on recess [91]. This can harm [92] children's mental health, causing increased anxiety and depression.

        1.1.4.3.4. Pro:
        Playing with other children stimulates brain development [93] and improves intelligence [94].

  1.2. Con:
  Mandatory schooling is too expensive.

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Mandatory education increases the involvement of state (pp. 13–14 [95]) and national (p. 4 [96]) governments in education, thereby reducing the economic efficiency of schools.

      1.2.1.1. Pro:
      Mandatory education is not held properly liable to market forces (p. 29 [97]); schooling should be privatized (p. 250 [98]).

        1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
        State regulations and funding restrictions have lowered the efficiency of schools [99]; this could be effectively combated through privatization.

        1.2.1.1.2. Con:
        The privatization of education, such as the establishment of charter schools, promotes socioeconomic and racial inequality (p. 4 [100]).

        1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
        Public schools in the US spend 53% more per student [101] than private schools.

        1.2.1.1.4. Con:
        Schooling could remain mandatory if all schools were privatized.

          1.2.1.1.4.1. Pro:
          Mandatory privatized schooling can be funded by giving students vouchers [102] to voluntarily choose which school they attend.

          1.2.1.1.4.2. Con:
          Mandatory privatized schooling would violate the establishment clause [103] of the US Constitution by supporting religious schools with public funds [104].

      1.2.1.2. Con:
      Mandatory schooling could be managed at a local level, reducing the inefficiencies associated with larger bureaucracies.

        1.2.1.2.1. Con:
        Localizing the administration of mandatory schooling would result in increased regional disparities in educational resources (pp. xiii, 94, 145 [105]).

        1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
        The localization of school administration promotes increased parent and community involvement (p. 43 [106]).

      1.2.1.3. Con:
      A national education system offers opportunities for broader initiatives to cut costs and improve efficiency.

        1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
        In 2022 [107], the White House allocated billions of dollars and released a toolkit to help schools become more energy efficient (p. 9 [108]).

        1.2.1.3.2. Con:
        Any cost savings measures suggested by the government are more than offset by the large increase in administrative costs [109] associated with a national system.

      1.2.1.4. Pro:
      The US spends an average of $11,763 [110] per student each year on mandatory public education. In 2016 [111], this amounted to $587 billion.

        1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
        US mandatory schooling is funded by property taxes, which unfairly forces childless people to fund the education of other people's children (pp. 1–2 [112]).

        1.2.1.4.2. Con:
        Many modern countries spend substantially less [113] than the US on mandatory schooling while achieving better academic results.

    1.2.2. Con:
    Primary and secondary schooling [114] is a necessity (pp. 12–13 [115]); it is normal for society to spend substantial resources on necessities.

      1.2.2.1. Pro:
      Mandatory education is necessary for society to guarantee a base level of citizen education and workforce readiness (pp. 4, 21 [116]).

        1.2.2.1.1. Con:
        This is a slippery slope argument [117]; if school were not mandatory, many people would still pursue education, as the potential for higher wages [118] creates a strong incentive to learn.

        1.2.2.1.2. Pro:
        Abolishing mandatory school without a suitable replacement would cause disruption to all institutions and industries that require an educated workforce in order to operate (pp. 8–9 [119]).

        1.2.2.1.3. Pro:
        Mandatory schooling increases [120] literacy rates, and a literate population is necessary for modern economies [121] to function.

          1.2.2.1.3.1. Con:
          Mandatory school does not necessarily result in a higher literacy rate. The literacy rate in Oman [122], where school is voluntary, is 95%, while the US, where school is mandatory, has a 79% literacy rate [123].

          1.2.2.1.3.2. Pro:
          Literacy is correlated with wealth, at both the individual level (p. 123 [124]) and the collective level of cities and nations (p. 8 [125]).

          1.2.2.1.3.3. Con:
          Humans may not need to be literate in the future if the majority of work is done by AI [126] and governments institute a universal basic income [127] system.

      1.2.2.2. Con:
      Mandatory schooling is not, strictly speaking, a necessity [128].

        1.2.2.2.1. Con:
        The nature of human necessity has shifted dramatically since the Industrial Revolution [129] and is driven by complex social requirements, such as those taught in school.

        1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
        Humans can survive without schooling and can choose to acquire an education voluntarily (p. 1 [130]).

        1.2.2.2.3. Con:
        Education is clearly linked to the human need for self-actualization [131]; making school mandatory ensures this need is addressed.

        1.2.2.2.4. Pro:
        There is no agreement on the purpose and function of schooling [132]. Therefore, making it mandatory seems inappropriate.

          1.2.2.2.4.1. Pro:
          The commonly proposed purposes of schooling [133]—academic discipline, socialization, and personal development—often conflict with each other.

          1.2.2.2.4.2. Con:
          Education is often a means to an end that seeks to improve a nation's productivity [134]. It is necessary that schooling is mandatory to fulfill this aim.

          1.2.2.2.4.3. Con:
          Many modern, Western schools are based on the theories of John Dewey, who considered the democratic education of children [135] to be the primary task of schooling.

      1.2.2.3. Pro:
      Education is essential to democratic societies (p. 94 [136]). If it were not mandatory, the values it instills could be more easily undermined.

        1.2.2.3.1. Con:
        The standardization of curricula associated with mandatory schooling is diametrically opposed to democratic ideals [137].

          1.2.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          A legally enforced state curriculum intrudes on the right of democratic citizens to freedom of thought (p. 1 [138]).

        1.2.2.3.2. Con:
        Mandatory school establishes compulsion as a legitimate means of pursuing a goal, which contradicts the values of democratic society (p. 171 [139]).

          1.2.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Compulsion by the state is an illegitimate use of power in a democracy unless used to avoid harm (pp. 91–92 [140]).

          1.2.2.3.2.2. Con:
          Students are never directly taught that compulsion is legitimate; there are nuances to understanding and following democratically mandated laws.

        1.2.2.3.3. Pro:
        Mandatory education is necessary to instill democratic ideals [141] in students and promote civic participation [142].

        1.2.2.3.4. Pro:
        Public education for the masses is necessary to protect democracy from demagogue rule [143].

          1.2.2.3.4.1. Con:
          Many public education students are incapable [144] of discerning between credible and fake news stories, leaving them vulnerable to demagogue rule.

          1.2.2.3.4.2. Pro:
          In Finland, critical thinking is part of the core curriculum, and students are taught how to identify fake news [145] as early as primary school. Other countries should follow this example.

        1.2.2.3.5. Pro:
        Schooling provides children with political awareness, which enables them to be responsible citizens (p. 4 [146]).

      1.2.2.4. Con:
      Despite being a necessity, the cost of the US mandatory school system is unnecessarily high (p. 3 [147]), in part driven up by fraud of up to $10.7 million [148] per year.

        1.2.2.4.1. Pro:
        Fraud reduces the efficiency of government spending and denies opportunities to others who could avail of the funds in different programs.

        1.2.2.4.2. Con:
        The amount of money lost to education fraud in the US is negligible, as the annual US education market is over $1.7 billion [149].

    1.2.3. Con:
    The long-term financial benefits of mandatory schooling far outweigh the costs to the state; investment in education can provide infinite economic return [150].

      1.2.3.1. Con:
      The available research on the rate of economic return of education suffers from methodological inconsistencies and sampling biases (p. 2 [151]).

        1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
        Payroll surveys are often completed by human resource departments, which skews [152] the distribution in favor of urban residents.

        1.2.3.1.2. Con:
        Numerous peer-reviewed academic studies have identified a significant return on investment [153] from education.

      1.2.3.2. Pro:
      The switch to compulsory education is associated with national economic improvement.

        1.2.3.2.1. Pro:
        Mandatory education in Singapore decreased reliance on low-paid, unskilled labor and increased investment in research and innovation (p. 160 [154]).

        1.2.3.2.2. Con:
        Despite instituting compulsory education [155] in 1990, Bangladesh remains among the world's poorest nations [156].

      1.2.3.3. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling creates an educated population (p. 19 [157]), which is vital to maintaining a skilled workforce [158] and increasing the tax base to guarantee future education funding.

        1.2.3.3.1. Con:
        Mandatory education is associated with, and has a role in, the birth of passive and dependent workers who uphold an exploitative capitalist system (pp. 29–30 [159]).

        1.2.3.3.2. Pro:
        The continued development of the economy depends on innovation, which is primarily accomplished by educated people, since they have more knowledge upon which to build (p. 32 [160]).

        1.2.3.3.3. Pro:
        There is a strong correlation [161] between education level and income, so those with more schooling are likely to have better paying jobs.

          1.2.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Those who complete mandatory education are more likely to be members of the middle class, who typically consume more and, thus, contribute more [162] to the economy.

          1.2.3.3.3.2. Pro:
          Many jobs require a bachelor's degree [163] in a particular subject, and advancement in many jobs requires at least an undergraduate degree [164]. Since one cannot attain such a degree without schooling, mandatory schooling should prepare all students for university education.

          1.2.3.3.3.3. Con:
          There are a limited number of high-paying jobs in any economy. Thus, mandatory schooling cannot ensure that everyone succeeds financially.

      1.2.3.4. Con:
      In East Asian countries, the high cost of tutoring and private schools [165] has contributed to low birth rates that threaten the long-term stability of the economy.

        1.2.3.4.1. Con:
        Participation in tutoring and cram schools is driven by a desire to gain a competitive edge [166] and is not inherently part of mandatory schooling.

        1.2.3.4.2. Pro:
        Persistent low birth rates can lead to a lack of workers and reduced tax revenue [167] in the future.

    1.2.4. Pro:
    A number of developing countries report an inability or unwillingness to bear the cost of compulsory education (pp. 79–80 [168]).

      1.2.4.1. Pro:
      Rates of student attendance and enrollment are as dependent upon the cost of attending school (p. 2 [169]) as on whether or not attendance is mandatory (p. 23 [170]). Instead of curtailing choice, states should make school free to attend.

        1.2.4.1.1. Pro:
        In Oman, school is voluntary [171] and free to attend [172], and enrollment is high, whereas in the Solomon Islands, school is mandatory [173] but not funded [174], and attendance remains low.

        1.2.4.1.2. Con:
        Mandatory schooling and free education do not preclude one another. Presenting the two as mutually exclusive creates a false dilemma [175].

      1.2.4.2. Pro:
      Shifting the cost burden of mandatory schooling to families prevents students of lower socioeconomic status from attending school (p. 29 [176]).

        1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
        Forcing families to pay for mandatory schooling contributes to intergenerational poverty as children of lower socioeconomic status are effectively prevented from improving their circumstances (pp. 3, 14 [177]).

        1.2.4.2.2. Con:
        Mandatory school has been demonstrated to be a key element in fighting poverty (pp. 599–600 [178]).

      1.2.4.3. Con:
      Most countries have mandatory schooling and do not seem to have a problem with shouldering the cost (p. 25 [179]).

        1.2.4.3.1. Pro:
        There are significant funds available to help developing countries implement compulsory education, originating from such organizations as the UN [180], OPEC [181], the World Bank [182], and USAID [183].

        1.2.4.3.2. Con:
        Some countries have ongoing structural issues that prevent mandatory schooling from being sufficiently funded (p. 80 [184]).

    1.2.5. Con:
    Mandatory schooling offers significant second-order benefits [185] to society.

      1.2.5.1. Pro:
      Education is correlated with better health [186] outcomes.

        1.2.5.1.1. Con:
        Since higher socioeconomic status is associated with both higher education levels and better health outcomes, it may be a common-causal variable [187] in the correlation between education and health.

        1.2.5.1.2. Pro:
        Idleness [188] in children is associated with lower levels of cognitive and social development, and negative health outcomes. In school, it is very difficult to be idle, which provides children an opportunity to build positive habits.

        1.2.5.1.3. Pro:
        Recess and physical education [189] classes may be the only physical exercise some students get, especially as less than 9% of teenagers get sufficient exercise (p. 1 [190]).

      1.2.5.2. Pro:
      Research suggests that there is a strong correlation between lower levels of education and criminal incarceration (p. 1 [191]) and that education reduces participation in criminal behavior (p. 183 [192]).

        1.2.5.2.1. Pro:
        Education may reduce crime by increasing the comparative profitability of noncriminal work (p. 155 [193]).

        1.2.5.2.2. Pro:
        Education may increase students' risk aversion to the legal repercussions of crime (p. 156 [194]).

        1.2.5.2.3. Con:
        Disadvantaged students are often [195] alienated [196] from school; this could drive such students to crime as a method of escape.

      1.2.5.3. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling lessens reliance on private childcare for working parents, allowing them to provide for their families without paying a large percentage of their wages for childcare (p. 40 [197]).

        1.2.5.3.1. Con:
        That mandatory schooling helps parents save money on childcare is an argument for free childcare [198] rather than mandatory education.

          1.2.5.3.1.1. Pro:
          There are a variety of options available for school-age child care [199] that could enable parents to work without the drawbacks of mandatory schooling.

          1.2.5.3.1.2. Con:
          Universal free childcare would be an enormous economic burden [200] on the state and would not provide the myriad additional benefits of mandatory schooling.

            1.2.5.3.1.2.1. Con:
            Universal free healthcare would not be a boon to the national economy, since it would allow all able bodied parents to work.

        1.2.5.3.2. Pro:
        On days when mandatory school is canceled, such as snow days [201], parents who cannot afford childcare are forced to choose between working and caring for their child.

      1.2.5.4. Con:
      Just because something is beneficial does not mean that it should be mandatory.

        1.2.5.4.1. Pro:
        An active and healthy populace is also beneficial to society, yet exercise is not compulsory.

      1.2.5.5. Con:
      Second-order effects are unpredictable, and often include "unknown unknowns [202]." There may be significant second-order disadvantages to mandatory schooling as well.

        1.2.5.5.1. Pro:
        The high value that mandatory schooling places on grades leads to grade inflation [203], which places it at odds with true academic achievement.

    1.2.6. Pro:
    Mandatory schooling creates a significant opportunity cost [204] for students in the form of the lost wages they could earn by entering the workforce earlier.

      1.2.6.1. Pro:
      Children that work rather than attending school can contribute needed resources to their family and learn valuable skills [205] at a young age.

        1.2.6.1.1. Con:
        Children may work because they are denied access [206] to free education due to their refugee status. Making schooling mandatory would require states to educate these children.

        1.2.6.1.2. Pro:
        Child abuse is pervasive [207] in schools in some countries; students may prefer to work to avoid these conditions and gain the relative stability that an income can afford them.

      1.2.6.2. Con:
      Mandatory schooling does not pose an opportunity cost to learners; instead, it mitigates the competitive advantage [208] enjoyed by those with a higher level of education.

        1.2.6.2.1. Pro:
        Child labor is exploitative [209] and robs children of their freedom, health, and future such that they cannot compete with their higher-income peers when they are older.

        1.2.6.2.2. Con:
        On-the-job professional development can greatly advance [210] one's career; beginning this at an earlier age would offer young people far more opportunities to advance in their chosen field.

      1.2.6.3. Con:
      Children's capacity to contribute to the economy is limited by a range of child labor laws [211].

        1.2.6.3.1. Pro:
        Mandatory schooling supports child labor laws by ensuring that children's time is verifiably spent on pursuits that can benefit them in the long term (p. 2 [212]).

        1.2.6.3.2. Con:
        Child labor laws and mandatory schooling are meaningless to families that cannot afford to eat [213]. In such cases, financial support for basic needs [214] is more likely to result in children attending school than making school mandatory.

        1.2.6.3.3. Con:
        Children in some nations can work, provided they give informed consent [215] and have the permission of a parent or guardian.

  1.3. Con:
  Mandatory schooling infringes upon the rights of children and their parents.

    1.3.1. Pro:
    Schools often violate students' rights [216] to free speech, free association, and privacy [217]. Making schooling mandatory subjects children to these conditions without their consent.

      1.3.1.1. Pro:
      In the US, the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act (FERPA [218]) allows schools to freely release public directory information about children, which includes personally identifiable information and creates a security risk.

        1.3.1.1.1. Con:
        Some schools circumvent FERPA requests by discouraging [219] the use of written records.

        1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
        FERPA records can include so-called "dirty data [220]" which can be actively detrimental to the success of marginalized students.

        1.3.1.1.3. Con:
        FERPA restricts the release of data to only those with a "legitimate educational interest [221]" and gives recourse to students when breaches occur.

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      Video surveillance [222] of students and monitoring [223] their chats and emails have become commonplace despite causing children discomfort [224] and posing a further risk of unauthorized access [225] to student information.

        1.3.1.2.1. Con:
        Given the epidemic of school violence [226] in the US, surveillance and monitoring are essential to protect students [227].

        1.3.1.2.2. Con:
        The use of surveillance technology to invade student privacy is better addressed by eliminating or changing these practices than by abolishing mandatory schooling.

        1.3.1.2.3. Pro:
        As students cannot vote [228], they are incapable of pursuing their civil rights. As such, encroachments into their privacy in the name of "protection" are likely to endure [229] in mandatory schooling systems.

        1.3.1.2.4. Pro:
        In some cases, monitoring of students has been extended to nonschool communications in support of questionable profiling policies [230] by law enforcement.

      1.3.1.3. Con:
      Since schools are public [231] rather than private environments, there is no reasonable expectation of privacy [232].

        1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
        The US Supreme Court has determined that there is a lowered expectation of privacy in schools [233]; for example, students are not entitled to the constitutional protection against unreasonable search and seizure.

        1.3.1.3.2. Con:
        Students remain entitled to a number of privacy protections [234] within schools.

        1.3.1.3.3. Con:
        Schools are public property [235] because they are owned by the state, but they are not open for unrestricted public access.

      1.3.1.4. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling undermines children's integrity and freedom (p. 65 [236]).

        1.3.1.4.1. Con:
        Allowing young children to choose to not develop intellectually is a violation of their integrity, as it forecloses future opportunities (p. 1989 [237]).

          1.3.1.4.1.1. Pro:
          School-age children often lack the Gillick competence necessary to make serious long-term decisions (p. 245 [238]), such as forgoing education.

            1.3.1.4.1.1.1. Con:
            With appropriate support, school-age children are capable of recognizing and advocating for their own learning needs (pp. 1–2 [239]). Infantalizing them contributes to the legitimization of their oppression (pp. 9–10 [240]).

            1.3.1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
            Children do not develop the capacity for long-term thinking until middle adolescence [241]. Mandatory school allows younger students to learn about the world under the guidance of trained professionals.

          1.3.1.4.1.2. Con:
          Children have a right to be involved in decisions about their future [242], even if they make bad decisions.

          1.3.1.4.1.3. Con:
          Allowing children greater freedom to make decisions can teach them responsibility and accountability [243].

            1.3.1.4.1.3.1. Con:
            There are better ways to teach children these lessons than allowing them to make life-altering decisions before they are ready (p. 5 [244]).

          1.3.1.4.1.4. Pro:
          As they lack experience and maturity, children are more likely to be short-termist [245] and cause irreversible damage [246] to their lives, e.g., by losing the opportunity for higher education.

        1.3.1.4.2. Pro:
        Students are often forced to spend time in a specific room, at a specific desk, doing specific things (p. 1 [247]). This extremely restrictive [248] practice suppresses their freedoms.

          1.3.1.4.2.1. Con:
          The ability to work in confined conditions for a long time is an essential skill in industrialized society (p. 1 [249]); schools teach this skill to prepare students for work.

          1.3.1.4.2.2. Pro:
          Forcing children to assume uncomfortable postures and restricting their movement undermines their sense of bodily autonomy [250].

          1.3.1.4.2.3. Pro:
          The disciplinary environment of mandatory schooling systems encourages [251] ill-informed expectations about the normal behavior [252] of school-age children, contributing to the overdiagnosis of attention disorders [253].

        1.3.1.4.3. Pro:
        Mandating school gives the state authority to define education in a way that benefits their interests [254], which could allow it to be used as a tool for indoctrination (p. 12 [255]).

          1.3.1.4.3.1. Pro:
          The American education system has been criticized for instilling children with consumerist ideology (p. 10 [256]).

          1.3.1.4.3.2. Pro:
          Under authoritarian regimes, such as the one in China [257], schools are often used to indoctrinate [258] children to idolize and blindly follow [259] political leaders.

          1.3.1.4.3.3. Con:
          Most democratic societies are unlikely to use education as a method of indoctrination, since such practices are diametrically opposed to the freedom of thought and conscience (pp. 525–526 [260]).

        1.3.1.4.4. Con:
        It is possible for mandatory schooling to happen in a generous and inclusive [261] way that preserves the freedom and individuality of children.

          1.3.1.4.4.1. Con:
          Most schools do not have the resources or time to ensure that the individual needs of each child are equally addressed (pp. 3–4 [262]).

            1.3.1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
            Programs that focus on the educational needs of individual students, such as the Expanded Learning Time (ELT) Initiative, have raised the annual cost of mandatory education by $1,400 to $5,000 per student (pp. 39, 45–47 [263]).

          1.3.1.4.4.2. Con:
          At its core, mandatory schooling endorses paternalism [264]. Even if it is framed in an inclusive manner, such schooling denies people the freedom to choose what is best for them.

          1.3.1.4.4.3. Pro:
          Some schools put very few limits on the freedom of the child, such as Sudbury schools [265], which operate on the idea of "freedom without license."

            1.3.1.4.4.3.1. Pro:
            Sudbury schools are less expensive [266] than the other private school [267] options.

              1.3.1.4.4.3.1.1. Con:
              Despite their cost being less than other private schools, the out-of-pocket cost of Sudbury schools places them out of reach for low-income families [268].

            1.3.1.4.4.3.2. Con:
            Sudbury schools are unlikely to become mainstream, as many people are uncomfortable [269] with their unorthodox approach to learning.

              1.3.1.4.4.3.2.1. Pro:
              Ensuring that the parents are on board with the philosophy tends to be an important part of the admissions process in Sudbury schools - precisely because it's bad for the students if they are not.

              1.3.1.4.4.3.2.2. Con:
              These opinions can change.

        1.3.1.4.5. Pro:
        Schooling can be used to coerce students into negative forms of cultural cohesion [270] that discourage dissent and favor the perspective of majority groups.

          1.3.1.4.5.1. Con:
          A mandatory common school system introduces all citizens to a common set of knowledge and competencies [271], making broad communication about complex subjects easier.

            1.3.1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
            The development of broad, general knowledge is an essential goal of democratic education (pp. 1, 3 [272]).

            1.3.1.4.5.1.2. Con:
            Schools can often have a biased [273] curriculum that promotes one demographic's point of view while suppressing the perspective of those who are not in power.

          1.3.1.4.5.2. Con:
          Schools, especially as they become increasingly diverse [274], allow children of different backgrounds to meet and become more aware of other cultures, which promotes multiculturalism and tolerance (p. 4 [275]).

            1.3.1.4.5.2.1. Pro:
            Cultural diversity in classrooms has continued to rise, and support for multicultural education has increased [276].

            1.3.1.4.5.2.2. Con:
            Schools only allow children from different backgrounds to interact when cohorts come from a wide area. However, most only draw from a limited community or district [277].

            1.3.1.4.5.2.3. Con:
            In the UK, working-class [278] children receive a lower-quality education and are placed in lower academic levels than middle-class peers in the same school.

            1.3.1.4.5.2.4. Pro:
            Cross-class friendships [279] help to mitigate class-based differences in student academic achievement.

              1.3.1.4.5.2.4.1. Pro:
              Diverse classrooms [280] have been shown to increase student achievement unilaterally and may better prepare students for changing demographics.

              1.3.1.4.5.2.4.2. Con:
              Children from wealthy families tend to attend school in wealthy districts [281], which reduces the chances that children from different class backgrounds will attend the same schools.

          1.3.1.4.5.3. Pro:
          It is difficult to distinguish between promoting some cultural ideas or rules and intentionally using schools to promote cultural homogeneity (p. 54 [282]).

          1.3.1.4.5.4. Pro:
          Schools in colonized areas have been used to suppress Indigenous cultures in favor of the imported cultures of European oppressors (pp. 30, 37 [283]).

            1.3.1.4.5.4.1. Con:
            In many former colonies, students are taught about colonial history and learn to think critically about culture and authority (p. 450 [284]).

            1.3.1.4.5.4.2. Pro:
            Forced schooling [285] of First Nation children in Canada, with the goal of assimilating them into Euro-Canadian society, separated families, undermined Indigenous culture, and resulted in horrendous abuse.

      1.3.1.5. Con:
      Students are not entitled to the same rights as adults, given that they are minors [286]. Instead, schools are empowered to act in loco parentis [287] to ensure the safety of students.

        1.3.1.5.1. Pro:
        Guardians [288] can overrule a child's right to privacy in the interest of their well-being.

        1.3.1.5.2. Con:
        The UN Human Rights Council has identified [289] privacy violations of children by adults as a key human rights issue.

      1.3.1.6. Con:
      Parents and children have certain protected rights [290] in public schools; making schooling mandatory ensures that more people are able to access these protections.

        1.3.1.6.1. Con:
        Schools have failed to protect the rights of students to learn in an environment that is free of bullying and sexual harassment (pp. 2–3 [291]).

        1.3.1.6.2. Pro:
        Schools can protect and promote the right to freedom of thought [292], belief, and religion. Making schooling mandatory further enshrines the importance of these values.

          1.3.1.6.2.1. Con:
          Some schools outlaw [293] or require [294] religious garb—both mandates deny students their freedom of religion.

          1.3.1.6.2.2. Pro:
          Mandatory schooling can introduce [295] students to a variety of faiths and philosophies, giving them greater capacity to decide which belief system is best for them.

          1.3.1.6.2.3. Pro:
          Mandatory schooling supports the rights of students to freedom of thought and belief by providing them with broad knowledge to assist them in developing their own views (pp. 5–6 [296]).

          1.3.1.6.2.4. Con:
          Teachers often instruct their students on political issues in ways that promote their political biases [297], undermining students' freedom of thought.

            1.3.1.6.2.4.1. Con:
            Education itself is inherently political [298]; avoiding contentious topics in the interest of neutrality does a disservice to students [299].

            1.3.1.6.2.4.2. Pro:
            Schools should teach about current events in an inclusive, nonpartisan [300] way that helps students develop their own opinions.

        1.3.1.6.3. Con:
        Mandatory schooling measures can contribute to the restriction of people's rights by encouraging overpolicing (p. 30 [301]).

          1.3.1.6.3.1. Pro:
          The criminalization of truancy does not increase [302] school attendance but instead overwhelmingly harms [303] low-income women.

          1.3.1.6.3.2. Pro:
          Overpolicing can have a number of drawbacks [304], including decreasing people's freedom by harming [305] their physical and mental health.

          1.3.1.6.3.3. Con:
          Truancy is correlated with higher rates of criminal behavior (p. 2 [306]); policing for truancy motivates students to respect the law.

          1.3.1.6.3.4. Con:
          Truancy contributes to schools receiving less funding [307]; the system should not suffer due to students' poor decisions.

        1.3.1.6.4. Pro:
        All laws related to children's health and safety apply in schools. This protects their bodily integrity [308] from a young age.

          1.3.1.6.4.1. Con:
          Many countries continue to allow corporal punishment in schools despite evidence that the practice is both physically and mentally harmful (p. 8 [309]).

          1.3.1.6.4.2. Con:
          School safety laws are developed at the state and local rather than national level [310] and can vary substantially.

          1.3.1.6.4.3. Pro:
          Health is a fundamental human right [311]—protecting it in schools ensures that students can access this right.

          1.3.1.6.4.4. Pro:
          The organized and monitored environment typical of schools makes it easier to prevent more serious violations of children's integrity and to quickly and efficiently address minor infractions.

    1.3.2. Con:
    Mandatory schooling supports the right of children to equal opportunity (p. 15 [312]).

      1.3.2.1. Con:
      There are a variety of external factors [313], such as home life, that affect the success of children in school (p. 1 [314]). Children who receive the same schooling are not necessarily on equal footing.

        1.3.2.1.1. Con:
        Schools mitigate some social inequalities, for example, by trying to ensure students receive adequate nutrition [315]. Without mandatory schooling, fewer children will be able to access these benefits.

        1.3.2.1.2. Pro:
        Research shows that the disparity [316] between rich and poor students' academic achievement has increased despite access to public education. Thus, mandatory schooling may not remediate inequalities of opportunity.

        1.3.2.1.3. Pro:
        Wealthy parents can afford for their children to have private tutors [317] and participate in extracurricular activities that give them an advantage over less privileged students.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      A basic level of education [318] is needed to ensure the future well-being of children.

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        Mandatory schooling provides a starting point for children to build ideas and gives them access to better employment options [319].

        1.3.2.2.2. Con:
        The future well-being of children is a secondary concern when their present well-being is at issue [320]; for example, millions of children suffer from hunger and starvation [321]. This is a more pressing concern than their education.

        1.3.2.2.3. Con:
        There have been illiterate people [322] throughout history who have made great contributions to society.

          1.3.2.2.3.1. Con:
          The existence of illiterate historical figures does not legitimize illiteracy, given that the majority of humanity's accomplishments in the modern era have been achieved by literate individuals (p. 84 [323]).

      1.3.2.3. Pro:
      Mandatory public schooling ensures that children from all backgrounds have access to similar educational resources.

        1.3.2.3.1. Con:
        Children from different socioeconomic backgrounds inherently have access to different educational resources (p. 16 [324]).

        1.3.2.3.2. Con:
        The US public education system reinforces existing disparities in wealth [325] through the unequal distribution of resources between schools.

          1.3.2.3.2.1. Con:
          Since universal education is strongly associated with reduced poverty and increased social mobility, getting rid of mandatory schooling would likely result in greater inequality than results from the unequal funding of schools (p. 6 [326]).

          1.3.2.3.2.2. Pro:
          Public schools in the US are funded unequally both because affluent areas benefit from higher property tax [327] revenues and because school districts vary significantly in size.

            1.3.2.3.2.2.1. Pro:
            Public schools located in elite areas have more resources [328] and provide children with a better education compared with schools in impoverished communities.

            1.3.2.3.2.2.2. Con:
            Unequal funding between schools and districts is not an argument against mandatory schooling but instead supports efforts to improve educational funding systems.

          1.3.2.3.2.3. Con:
          Since the cost of education [329] may be outside the means of impoverished families, mandatory and free education helps counteract inequality by ensuring their children are not locked out of learning.

          1.3.2.3.2.4. Pro:
          School districts in impoverished areas have more students in need of help yet less available resources. Lower per-student spending can irreparably damage poor children's futures. [330]

            1.3.2.3.2.4.1. Con:
            The abolition of mandatory school would be more harmful than substandard schooling to impoverished students who chose to no longer attend school.

            1.3.2.3.2.4.2. Pro:
            Poor facilities and resources [331] affect both a teacher's ability to teach and a student's ability to learn.

            1.3.2.3.2.4.3. Pro:
            Disparities in resources and funding lead to achievement gaps, so students in high-poverty areas have lower test scores, increased absences, and later graduation dates (p. 119 [332]).

        1.3.2.3.3. Pro:
        When school is compulsory, children with lower socioeconomic status gain access to educational resources they might otherwise be unable to obtain; this helps counteract social inequality (p. 27 [333]).

          1.3.2.3.3.1. Con:
          Even when school is mandatory, students from disadvantaged backgrounds show lower academic achievement [334] relative to their peers.

          1.3.2.3.3.2. Pro:
          Poor children often lack access to resources, such as school supplies, computers, and books [335], which they can access via compulsory schooling.

      1.3.2.4. Con:
      Addressing inequalities does not necessitate mandatory schooling. Young people who engage voluntarily with school are best positioned to benefit from it (p. 103 [336]).

        1.3.2.4.1. Con:
        Even students who are forced to attend school gain benefits, which directly acts to mitigate educational inequality. Allowing students to opt-in to school only privileges those with parents who place a high premium on education.

        1.3.2.4.2. Pro:
        Resources are better spent on students with a desire to learn; it is wasteful and inefficient to educate people against their will.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    Mandatory schooling can easily infringe upon parents' right to raise their children in accordance with their family's social and/or religious paradigms (p. 861 [337]).

      1.3.3.1. Con:
      Educators and social institutions are better positioned than parents to make educational decisions on behalf of children (p. 3 [338]).

        1.3.3.1.1. Con:
        Since parents are more familiar with their children's needs [339] than those who make educational decisions (pp. 5, 22 [340]), they should decide whether school will best serve their child.

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        The majority of parents are not qualified to teach a single subject (p. 14 [341]), much less understand how curricula and educational objectives are developed and implemented (pp. 3–4 [342]).

      1.3.3.2. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling often exposes children to topics that are controversial or contrary to the societal or religious beliefs of their parents (p. 1 [343]).

        1.3.3.2.1. Pro:
        Individual schools and teachers can espouse a different set of values [344] from those that parents wish to foster.

          1.3.3.2.1.1. Con:
          Mandatory schooling can include both secular and religious schools; parents who want their children to be educated with a particular set of values can send their children to private religious schools [345].

        1.3.3.2.2. Con:
        Exposure to controversial topics and opinions supports the development of critical thinking and helps children make up their own minds rather than blindly following the status quo (pp. 56, 57 [346]).

          1.3.3.2.2.1. Pro:
          Children whose parents hold fundamentalist beliefs would be at a strong disadvantage in society without such exposure (pp. 23–24 [347]). For instance, a teenager could become pregnant because their religious parents refused to educate them on birth control and preached abstinence instead.

          1.3.3.2.2.2. Con:
          Children are often strongly influenced [348] by their teachers and may abandon their own critical thinking efforts to agree with them. As this could threaten a child's religious or social upbringing, parents should be able to refuse to send them to school.

        1.3.3.2.3. Con:
        Children can easily be taught about a range of different topics while still adhering to their family's social or religious paradigm (p. 280 [349]).

          1.3.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          Learning about religion does not make students religious, just as being exposed to sex education does not make students become sexually active.

        1.3.3.2.4. Pro:
        Many parents are opposed to topics such as sex education [350] or global warming [351] being taught in school (p. 164 [352]). Other topics, such as evolution and creationism [353], explicitly contradict specific religious doctrines.

          1.3.3.2.4.1. Con:
          Homeschooling allows parents to introduce topics in ways that conform to their social or religious beliefs [354] while ensuring that children achieve an acceptable level of competency in basic subjects.

      1.3.3.3. Con:
      Children's right to education [355] trumps any purported right of parents to keep them ignorant or indoctrinated.

        1.3.3.3.1. Pro:
        The constitutional right [356] of parents to direct their child's education does not include the right to forgo their education.

        1.3.3.3.2. Con:
        The interest of the state in the schooling of children does not reasonably extend beyond ensuring that they receive a basic level of education (pp. 901–902 [357]).

        1.3.3.3.3. Con:
        In Pierce v. Society of Sisters [358], the US Supreme Court ruled that parents have the right to direct their child's education.

        1.3.3.3.4. Pro:
        Parents can be punished for educational neglect [359] if they refuse to allow their children to receive an education.

    1.3.4. Con:
    All laws infringe upon some aspect of human freedom, but society must determine whether the benefits outweigh such restrictions. A compulsory education is clearly more beneficial [360] than a lifetime of ignorance.

      1.3.4.1. Con:
      Education can be accomplished without making school mandatory.

        1.3.4.1.1. Pro:
        Voluntary schooling supports the individuality of students [361] and is more effective at teaching them how to make positive contributions to society.

        1.3.4.1.2. Pro:
        Unschoolers [362] eschew traditional schools to encourage children's curiosity with self-driven learning. Many students [363] educated in this manner have found it effective [364].

          1.3.4.1.2.1. Con:
          There is likely a sampling bias in studies of unschoolers; parents who opt for the method are more likely to be attentive, wealthy, and engaged in motivating their children to learn (p. 112 [365]).

          1.3.4.1.2.2. Pro:
          Subjects like math [366] and biology [367] are now easy to learn outside of school on platforms like YouTube [368], as are creative skills [369].

          1.3.4.1.2.3. Pro:
          Opting out of mandatory classes and focusing on remote learning has given some higher-achieving students more autonomy [370], allowing them to acquire knowledge quickly.

        1.3.4.1.3. Con:
        The ability to learn outside of traditional schooling does not demonstrate that unschooling is effective, especially as some unschooled children lack basic reading and mathematics skills [371].

      1.3.4.2. Pro:
      The lack of access to education for youth in developing countries has become a security issue, as ignorance can contribute to extremism [372].

        1.3.4.2.1. Con:
        Radicalization relies on more than just ignorance; the bullying [373] some children encounter at school can make them more vulnerable to extremism.

        1.3.4.2.2. Pro:
        Ignorance [374] enables racism and bigotry, and ignorance of other cultures leads to harmful nationalism [375].

  1.4. Pro:
  Mandatory schooling promotes the academic and cognitive development of children.

    1.4.1. Pro:
    An increase in the number of years of school completed is associated with an increase in intelligence (p. 1366 [376]).

      1.4.1.1. Con:
      Intelligence is culturally specific [377]; many forms of intelligence are ignored by modern testing methods.

        1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
        Schools that fail to account for the different types of intelligence [378] do a disservice to students and can easily disenfranchise them with education.

        1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
        Intelligence testing methods can contain ethnocultural bias that places minority students at a disadvantage (p. 1 [379]); the skewed results of such assessments have been used to support notions of racial essentialism [380].

        1.4.1.1.3. Con:
        Education is also culturally specific [381]; mandatory schooling in different cultures can focus on forms of intelligence that their society values more.

        1.4.1.1.4. Con:
        Schools teach [382] the values of the community. Even if school were not mandatory, students would be unlikely to escape their community's narrow definition of intelligence.

      1.4.1.2. Pro:
      Compulsory school laws (CSL) increase school attendance and enrollment (p. 15 [383]), increasing the likelihood that students will hone their intelligence.

        1.4.1.2.1. Con:
        Graduation rates are unaffected by increasing the mandatory school attendance age (p. 7 [384]).

        1.4.1.2.2. Pro:
        Up to 25% of potential dropouts remain in school due to compulsory education laws (pp. 1–2 [385]).

        1.4.1.2.3. Pro:
        Compulsory education is associated with increased educational attainment (p. 3 [386]).

      1.4.1.3. Pro:
      Mandatory schooling ensures students have the opportunity to develop critical thinking [387] skills, enabling them to effectively reason, make decisions, and solve problems.

        1.4.1.3.1. Pro:
        By studying humanities subjects, such as history [388] and English [389], children learn to critically analyze texts, construct narratives, and judge the value of evidence. These skills are widely applicable in broader society [390].

        1.4.1.3.2. Con:
        Some educational policies and practices have hindered [391] schools from effectively teaching critical thinking skills (p. 11 [392]).

          1.4.1.3.2.1. Pro:
          Some believe that Australian curricula need to be updated to better embed [393] critical thinking and improve student educational and career outcomes.

        1.4.1.3.3. Con:
        75% of employers believe that the people they hire lack critical thinking skills [394] despite these employees having completed years of formal education.

        1.4.1.3.4. Pro:
        Schooling encourages fact-based problem-solving [395] and analysis; making it mandatory affords children more time in which to hone these skills.

          1.4.1.3.4.1. Con:
          This kind of education would be exemplified by teaching students logic; however, formal logic is not part of the curriculum [396] in most schools.

          1.4.1.3.4.2. Pro:
          Developing problem-solving skills has been shown [397] to increase children's confidence and independence, which enables them to make better decisions.

    1.4.2. Con:
    Schools often utilize ineffective educational methods that detract from learning (p. 2 [398]).

      1.4.2.1. Pro:
      Many schools employ instructional methods based on the learning styles [399] of students despite evidence that this practice is ineffective [400].

        1.4.2.1.1. Pro:
        Even when provided with clear evidence that teaching methods based on Learning Styles Theory are ineffective, one-third of educators indicated that they would continue to use such methods (p. 8 [401]).

          1.4.2.1.1.1. Con:
          While Learning Styles Theory (LST) is flawed, students will still have different kinds of personalities and interests, and thus still prefer different kinds of instruction - varied instruction has also been shown to be motivating for students in it self. It is possible that teachers continue using methods inspired by LST because they're noticing these benefits.

        1.4.2.1.2. Con:
        Some research suggests that the incorporation of multiple learning styles [402] into teaching methods can improve student outcomes.

      1.4.2.2. Con:
      Teachers are trained professionals with specific competencies, such as a working knowledge of pedagogical models, that make them well suited to educating children (p. 150 [403]).

        1.4.2.2.1. Con:
        The pedagogical methods used by teachers are evaluated using biased standards [404]. Thus, they may not reflect the best way to learn.

          1.4.2.2.1.1. Pro:
          According to Goodhart's Law [405], when any measure becomes a target, it loses its value as a measure. This may apply to educational outcomes and lead to biased evaluation standards.

        1.4.2.2.2. Pro:
        Governments have an incentive to ensure that teachers are well-trained [406], as poor education practices weaken the skills of the future workforce [407].

          1.4.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          Some nations, such as Singapore, promote mastery [408] in particular subjects. The success [409] of such efforts has led to their wider adoption [410], demonstrating the drive to improve mandatory schooling.

        1.4.2.2.3. Con:
        Teachers are often hesitant [411] to adopt new methods of teaching common skills, such as reading, as they are not given enough support. This contributes to a significant, perennial lack of student competency [412].

      1.4.2.3. Pro:
      Mandatory school systems leave little to no room for individual learning paths and focus on standardization [413], which creates a number of problems [414].

        1.4.2.3.1. Con:
        The standardization of education ensures that all children are educated appropriately and have equal opportunities [415] to learn.

          1.4.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          Standardized testing is the most fair and equitable [416] way to compare different students.

          1.4.2.3.1.2. Con:
          Research has shown that standardized test scores are heavily influenced [417] by family income, with wealthier students often scoring higher than students from low-income families.

          1.4.2.3.1.3. Pro:
          Standardization ensures that students who change schools [418] do not lose the opportunity to learn essential skills and information.

          1.4.2.3.1.4. Con:
          Standardized testing marginalizes [419] some student populations and has often been invoked as support for ethnocentric and eugenicist beliefs about intelligence.

        1.4.2.3.2. Pro:
        Modern society is highly specialized [420] and requires specialized, not standardized, workers and citizens.

          1.4.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Society is entering an era of hyperspecialization [421], which has improved the quality and efficiency of products, as different tasks can be completed by people who are particularly skilled at what they're doing.

            1.4.2.3.2.1.1. Con:
            Having an array of general skills and varied experiences [422] may set one up for greater success in life than being overly specialized.

          1.4.2.3.2.2. Con:
          Specialization is more appropriate in higher education. Forcing children to specialize when they haven't fully explored their talents and preferences can lock them into pursuing a career they might later regret [423].

        1.4.2.3.3. Pro:
        Many teachers teach to the test [424] and focus on achieving high standardized test scores rather than allowing their students to truly master topics and skills.

          1.4.2.3.3.1. Pro:
          Children are often spoon-fed information at school, which they are only expected to memorize and reproduce [425] on standardized exams.

            1.4.2.3.3.1.1. Con:
            Teaching certain concepts and subjects, including languages, necessarily relies heavily on rote learning (p. 987 [426]).

            1.4.2.3.3.1.2. Pro:
            Rote learning [427] does not promote a deep, meaningful understanding of a subject.

          1.4.2.3.3.2. Con:
          Standardized tests can be used as a form of both academic and nonacademic assessment [428] to support students who need it the most.

        1.4.2.3.4. Con:
        Standardization makes it easier to implement [429] educational initiatives on a national scale, allowing mandatory education to benefit as many children as possible.

          1.4.2.3.4.1. Con:
          The US government is prohibited [430] from standardizing the curricula, materials, or methods utilized by schools; such initiatives are under the authority of state and local [431] school boards.

          1.4.2.3.4.2. Pro:
          Standarization is common [432] in many countries throughout the world, suggesting that it is an effective and efficient way to educate students.

            1.4.2.3.4.2.1. Pro:
            The US [433] and the UK [434] have legislation and standardization that define learning goals.

            1.4.2.3.4.2.2. Con:
            The parent claim makes a fallacious appeal to popularity [435]; the prevalence of a practice is not evidence of its effectiveness or efficiency.

        1.4.2.3.5. Pro:
        Standardization does not accurately reflect the nuances of human intelligence and learning ability (pp. 45–46 [436]).

          1.4.2.3.5.1. Con:
          Although it is important for educators to cultivate an understanding of students' unique abilities, schools must also rely on objective measures [437], like standardized tests, to examine larger groups and promote uniform outcomes.

          1.4.2.3.5.2. Pro:
          Many skills like creativity and initiative are not taught in schools, as they are not easily assessed using standardized tests [438].

        1.4.2.3.6. Con:
        There are ways to meet educational standards [439] without teaching in a standardized way.

          1.4.2.3.6.1. Con:
          Educational standards that do not include some degree of standardization are too variable to be useful (p. 9 [440]).

          1.4.2.3.6.2. Pro:
          Inclusive schools [441] that adapt their instructional methods to the needs of each individual child can allow a diverse range of students the opportunity to flourish.

    1.4.3. Con:
    As children are likely to naturally learn [442] and grow as they age, mandatory schooling is detrimental to their development because it undermines their desire [443] to learn.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      Schools can use student-centered curricula [444] to increase student engagement and ensure they learn more.

        1.4.3.1.1. Pro:
        The Montessori [445] teaching method allows students greater control over the subjects they study.

        1.4.3.1.2. Con:
        Given that teachers are already overworked [446] in many countries, providing students with bespoke resources based on their individual interests may prove too demanding for many faculties.

        1.4.3.1.3. Pro:
        Teachers can help children become autonomous learners [447] who are responsible for their own learning.

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Self-directed education supports children's natural curiosity [448] better than mandatory schooling.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        Children know [449] what methods of learning work best for them and should be left to their own devices.

        1.4.3.2.2. Con:
        Children's natural curiosity alone is insufficient for acquiring more detailed knowledge, which requires the development of noncognitive skills, including self-control, persistence, and self-discipline (p. 128 [450]).

        1.4.3.2.3. Con:
        Self-directed learning is incredibly difficult [451] as individuals lack the external accountability structures and competition of traditional schooling.

          1.4.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          The grading systems of traditional schooling provide students with external motivation by highlighting their progress and deficiencies (p. 21 [452]).

          1.4.3.2.3.2. Con:
          Despite its difficulties, self-directed learning is associated with more positive academic outcomes (pp. 10–11 [453]).

          1.4.3.2.3.3. Pro:
          Students with ADHD may benefit from body doubling [454], which is possible in classrooms but difficult to replicate in self-directed learning.

        1.4.3.2.4. Pro:
        Plato dismissed compulsory education and instead recommended encouraging students to pursue what amuses their minds [455] to help them realize their potential.

          1.4.3.2.4.1. Pro:
          Inquiry-based learning increases student engagement (p. 19 [456]).

          1.4.3.2.4.2. Con:
          Although children will naturally learn about things that intrigue them, they require the guidance and assistance of parents and educators [457] to identify gaps in their knowledge.

            1.4.3.2.4.2.1. Pro:
            Mandatory schooling ensures that topics are structured [458] so that each lesson builds upon prior concepts, which enables students to fulfill their potential.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      In mandatory school systems, teachers often avoid effective methods to promote student motivation, such as the autonomy-supportive intervention program (ASIP) (p. 130 [459]), because they seem too difficult to implement (p. 179 [460]).

        1.4.3.3.1. Con:
        While support for student autonomy in the classroom improves student motivation [461], there is no evidence to suggest that motivation would continue to increase outside the classroom.

        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:
        Voluntary schooling would be more autonomy-supportive than mandatory schooling and would preserve student interest and curiosity (p. 79 [462]).

        1.4.3.3.3. Con:
        Studies on the obstacles preventing teachers from instituting autonomy-supportive methods are largely based on self-reported, subjective factors (p. 187 [463]); such personal factors may not generalize to the mandatory school system as a whole.

      1.4.3.4. Con:
      Children with a natural desire to learn may lack the means to do so [464] if schooling is not made mandatory.

        1.4.3.4.1. Con:
        Children with the desire to learn will find a way to do so whether they attend school or not; this is demonstrated by the success of many well-known self-educated people [465].

        1.4.3.4.2. Pro:
        Mandatory schooling ensures that children have access to information and activities that enable their educational development (p. 5 [466]).

          1.4.3.4.2.1. Con:
          Children can access the same information they receive in schools through other means, such as community mentorship or public library programs [467] (p. 1 [468]).

          1.4.3.4.2.2. Pro:
          Even in developed countries, a sizeable percentage [469] of households do not have internet access. A school may be the only place where many students can learn how to use the internet and improve their technological literacy [470].

    1.4.4. Pro:
    Mandatory schooling ensures that students learn a wide range of subjects [471] and ideas to which they might not otherwise be exposed.

      1.4.4.1. Pro:
      Younger children may consider subjects like mathematics dull [472] and might be unlikely to pursue them on their own.

        1.4.4.1.1. Con:
        Many children dislike mathematics due to social conditioning [473]. If parents were more encouraging, children might pursue the subject more often.

        1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
        Subjects like mathematics force students to employ more complex thought processes and therefore require additional patience [474]. Mandatory education gives students an incentive to persevere that they may otherwise lack in self-directed study.

        1.4.4.1.3. Con:
        Experiences at schools without mandatory class attendance, like Summerhill and Sudbury Valley, show that younger children will still usually choose to go to these classes

      1.4.4.2. Con:
      Learning outside of the classroom [475] can better help students adapt to new environments and be exposed to diverse, real-world experiences.

        1.4.4.2.1. Pro:
        Forcing children to go to school prevents them from accessing many social and educational resources (e.g. museum exhibitions, neighborhood events) that can contribute to their education.

          1.4.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          A growing number of museums offer hands-on learning opportunities in academic subjects [476], which are better suited to the learning needs of children.

          1.4.4.2.1.2. Con:
          Children can still visit these social resources on school trips [477], on their holidays, or on the weekends.

        1.4.4.2.2. Pro:
        Many of the required classes and subjects in mandatory school curricula do not add [478] meaningfully to students' professional development as they are only useful in rare circumstances [479].

          1.4.4.2.2.1. Con:
          Many schools now offer computer science and coding [480] classes to help prepare children for the modern work environment.

            1.4.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
            Many [481] countries [482] suffer from a lack of computer science teachers.

          1.4.4.2.2.2. Con:
          Failing to grasp the basic concepts of subjects like chemistry [483], physics [484], and language arts [485] would hinder a student's understanding of numerous facets of everyday life, making communication at work more difficult.

            1.4.4.2.2.2.1. Pro:
            Language arts is one of the most important subjects, as it teaches students communication and soft skills [486] that they will apply in whichever career they choose.

          1.4.4.2.2.3. Pro:
          Mandatory school curricula do not include coursework in essential life skills [487], such as financial literacy and business networking, which would benefit students' professional development.

            1.4.4.2.2.3.1. Con:
            Many schools offer career services [488] that teach students about business networking, financial literacy, and job searches.

            1.4.4.2.2.3.2. Pro:
            Having knowledge about personal finance has a positive effect on a person's overall wealth (p. 21 [489]).

          1.4.4.2.2.4. Pro:
          Many elements of mandatory high school curricula are designed to prepare students for college, but this does not serve the interests or needs of all students (pp. i, iii [490]).

            1.4.4.2.2.4.1. Pro:
            The careers of some students would benefit more from technical and vocational [491] education, especially as careers in the trades can have a high salary potential.

            1.4.4.2.2.4.2. Con:
            -> See 1.2.3.3.3.2.

            1.4.4.2.2.4.3. Con:
            Students cannot predetermine how their professional journeys [492] will progress. A solid curriculum prepares them for many possible careers by giving them a robust array of skills.

            1.4.4.2.2.4.4. Pro:
            As colleges and universities [493] become more expensive [494], schools should only prepare the most academically inclined students for academic careers.

        1.4.4.2.3. Con:
        Field trips [495] are a common activity at many schools and allow students to continue their learning outside of the classroom.

        1.4.4.2.4. Con:
